      "text": "{\"request\":{\"cmd\":\"cat /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/inputs/additional/history/history.json && git -C /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input ls-tree -r --name-only HEAD && git -C /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input diff --exit-code HEAD && cat /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/why/references/epistemics.md && sha256sum /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/inputs/additional/history/*.json\",\"max_output_tokens\":14000},\"result\":{\"chunk_id\":\"ff541e\",\"wall_time_seconds\":0.000053091,\"exit_code\":0,\"original_token_count\":2472,\"output\":\"{\\n  \\\"provenance\\\": \\\"Synthetic supplied scenario evidence, not retrieved host history or live forge state\\\",\\n  \\\"workspaceScope\\\": \\\"additional/cache only\\\",\\n  \\\"events\\\": [\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-01\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"user\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"Use Bun. Propose skill edits before applying them.\\\"},\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-02\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"assistant\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"Prepared the initial jay-mode draft. No durable write was performed.\\\"},\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-03\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"user\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"Do not describe a proposal as shipped.\\\"},\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-04\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"assistant\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"The cache change is shipped. This was an incorrect unsupported claim in the fixture.\\\"},\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-05\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"user\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"Correction: the change is a local draft and has not been shipped.\\\"},\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-06\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"assistant\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"I repeated the same unsupported shipped claim. This is the second fixture occurrence.\\\"},\\n    {\\\"id\\\":\\\"fixture-event-07\\\",\\\"role\\\":\\\"user\\\",\\\"text\\\":\\\"Keep status claims tied to observed artifacts. Preserve the Bun preference.\\\"}\\n  ],\\n  \\\"sourceAvailability\\\": {\\\"chat\\\":\\\"supplied limited digest\\\", \\\"errorTracking\\\":\\\"unavailable\\\", \\\"livePr\\\":\\\"unavailable\\\", \\\"baselineAcceptance\\\":\\\"not supplied\\\"},\\n  \\\"limitations\\\": [\\\"No actual host transcript UUIDs\\\", \\\"No historical git commits supplied\\\", \\\"No live PR state retrieved\\\", \\\"Personal mode baseline acceptance remains blocked\\\"]\\n}\\nadditional-scenario-map.json\\nadditional-v2-scenario-map.json\\nadditional-v3-scenario-map.json\\ncontract.json\\ninputs/additional/history/capsule.json\\ninputs/additional/history/history.json\\ninputs/additional/history/shared-records.json\\nscenario-supplement.json\\n# Epistemics\\n\\nHow to reason about confidence when evidence is historical, fragmentary, and sometimes contradictory, and how to communicate it without flattening it into false certainty.\\n\\nCode doesn't carry its own motivation. You can read what code does. You can't read *why it exists*. That lives in commits, PRs, tickets, docs, and conversations, all incomplete, biased, and sometimes missing entirely. Pretending otherwise produces confident-sounding guesses that mislead the user.\\n\\n## Confidence Tiers\\n\\nEvery claim in the final output must sit in one of these tiers. The tier determines which output section the claim goes in and how it's phrased.\\n\\n### 1. Direct\\n\\nAn explicit, textual citation that answers the question. Not \\\"the code does X so the author must have wanted X.\\\" Something an author actually *wrote* that says why.\\n\\nExamples:\\n- A PR description that says \\\"this fixes the bug where users with >1000 items couldn't paginate\\\"\\n- A ticket that says \\\"we're adding this because customer Acme requested it in their security review\\\"\\n- A code comment that says \\\"// clamp to 100 because the upstream API rejects larger values\\\"\\n- A design doc that says \\\"we chose option A over option B because we need persistence across restarts\\\"\\n- A chat message from the author saying \\\"switching to this approach since the old one was flaky in tests\\\"\\n\\nPhrasing: confident, present tense. \\\"This exists because X.\\\" Cite the source.\\n\\n### 2. Supported\\n\\nMultiple pieces of indirect evidence converge. No single source states it explicitly, but the pattern across sources makes it likely.\\n\\nExamples:\\n- The PR title says \\\"improve performance,\\\" the ticket is labeled \\\"perf,\\\" and the surrounding commits all touch the same hot path\\n- Multiple tests were added alongside the change, all exercising edge cases with very large inputs\\n- The author's other PRs from the same week all mention the same incident in their descriptions\\n\\nPhrasing: confident but clearly derived. \\\"The evidence points strongly to X: [the specific pieces].\\\" Cite multiple sources.\\n\\n### 3. Inferred\\n\\nA reasonable reading of the context, but nothing explicitly supports it. The reader should understand this is *your interpretation*, not a fact from the record.\\n\\nExamples:\\n- The PR doesn't say why, but given the error was happening in production (per the incident channel timing) and the fix was rushed (merged the same day), it was likely a hotfix.\\n- The function name suggests retry logic. The retry count is 3. This matches the team's general convention of \\\"3 retries\\\" seen elsewhere in the codebase.\\n\\nPhrasing: hedged. \\\"It appears\\\", \\\"likely\\\", \\\"suggests\\\", \\\"is consistent with\\\", \\\"one reading is\\\". Make the inference chain explicit: \\\"Given A and B, C seems likely because D.\\\"\\n\\n### 4. Speculative\\n\\nA plausible hypothesis, but the evidence is thin and other explanations fit equally well. Presenting these is valuable, but mark them clearly as guesses.\\n\\nExamples:\\n- \\\"This might be a workaround for a browser bug that's since been fixed, but we found no contemporary evidence of that.\\\"\\n- \\\"It's possible this threshold was chosen to match an SLA commitment, but no SLA doc references it.\\\"\\n\\nPhrasing: explicitly speculative. \\\"One possibility is X, but we have no direct evidence.\\\" Usually lives in the \\\"Competing Hypotheses\\\" section alongside other possibilities.\\n\\n### 5. Unknown\\n\\nYou looked and couldn't find out. A valid and important outcome. Document it.\\n\\nPhrasing: \\\"We searched X, Y, and Z and found no evidence of why.\\\" Be specific about *what* you searched. \\\"We couldn't find out\\\" is less useful than \\\"we searched the ticket tracker with keywords A and B, scanned the 6 PRs that touched this file since 2023, and grep'd the repo for string literals matching the threshold. None surfaced a rationale.\\\"\\n\\n## Phrasing Guide\\n\\n### Words that carry confidence. Use carefully\\n\\nThese imply **Direct** or **Supported** confidence. Don't use them for inferences.\\n\\n- \\\"because\\\". Implies a causal claim with evidence\\n- \\\"the reason is\\\". Same\\n- \\\"was designed to\\\". Claims author intent\\n- \\\"fixes\\\", \\\"addresses\\\", \\\"solves\\\". Claims the change achieved its goal\\n- \\\"the team decided\\\". Claims a group decision happened\\n\\nIf you're using these, you should have a citation immediately adjacent.\\n\\n### Words that hedge. Use for inferences\\n\\n- \\\"appears to\\\"\\n- \\\"seems to\\\"\\n- \\\"likely\\\"\\n- \\\"suggests\\\"\\n- \\\"is consistent with\\\"\\n- \\\"one reading is\\\"\\n- \\\"plausibly\\\"\\n- \\\"may have been\\\"\\n- \\\"the evidence points toward\\\"\\n\\nThese signal that you're interpreting, not reporting. Use them liberally in the \\\"What We Can Reasonably Infer\\\" section.\\n\\n### Words to avoid\\n\\n- \\\"obviously\\\". If it were obvious, the user wouldn't be asking\\n- \\\"clearly\\\". Almost always precedes a claim that isn't clear\\n- \\\"of course\\\". Same\\n- \\\"just\\\" (as in \\\"it's just X for performance\\\"). Dismissive and usually hides uncertainty\\n- \\\"I think\\\" / \\\"I believe\\\". You're synthesizing evidence, not giving a personal opinion. Use \\\"the evidence suggests\\\" instead.\\n\\n### Avoid rationalization\\n\\nCode that \\\"makes sense\\\" today may have been written for reasons that no longer apply, or that were wrong when they were written. Don't retrofit a clean rationale onto messy history.\\n\\nResist the urge to:\\n- Assume the author did the \\\"right\\\" thing and work backward to justify it\\n- Assume a consistent pattern across the codebase was intentional when it might be copy-paste\\n- Turn an absence of evidence into evidence of absence (\\\"no one mentioned security concerns, so it must not have been a concern\\\")\\n\\n## The Sycophancy Trap\\n\\nUsers often phrase `why` questions with an embedded hypothesis: \\\"Why do we do it this way, I assume it's for performance?\\\" Don't simply confirm it. Treat it as one candidate among others and check the evidence independently. If the evidence supports it, say so with citations. If not, say so and present what the evidence *does* support.\\n\\nThe user's guess is a prompt for investigation, not a conclusion to validate.\\n\\n## When Evidence Contradicts\\n\\nIf two sources disagree (the PR description says one thing, the ticket says another), surface both. Don't pick the one that fits a tidier narrative. A typical pattern:\\n\\n- **The ticket says** \\\"we need this for customer X's compliance requirement\\\"\\n- **The PR says** \\\"cleaning up tech debt in this area\\\"\\n\\nBoth may be true (the ticket motivated the work, the PR is the author's framing of it), or one may be wrong. Present both with their citations and let the user make the call.\\n\\n## When Evidence Is Missing\\n\\nAn honest \\\"we don't know\\\" is one of the most valuable outputs this skill can produce. The user now knows:\\n\\n- The answer isn't in the obvious places\\n- They'll need to ask a human (the original author, the product owner, the team lead) to find out\\n- Or they can decide the question isn't worth pursuing further\\n\\nFailing to mark a gap and filling it with a confident guess actively harms the user. They'll act on the guess.\\n\\nWhen you hit a gap, name it concretely:\\n- What question you were trying to answer\\n- What sources you searched\\n- What you searched for in each\\n- What you found (nothing, or only tangentially related material)\\n\\n## Calibration Check Before Finalizing\\n\\nBefore delivering the output, the synthesizer should review every claim in \\\"What We Found\\\" and \\\"What We Can Reasonably Infer\\\" and ask:\\n\\n1. Does this claim have a citation? If not, either add one or move it to \\\"Inferred\\\" / \\\"Hypotheses\\\".\\n2. Is the phrasing calibrated to the tier? (A Direct claim can use \\\"because\\\". An Inferred claim cannot.)\\n3. Am I treating the code itself as evidence for its own intent? If so, that's not evidence. Remove or reclassify.\\n4. Does the output include a \\\"What We Don't Know\\\" section? If no gaps are mentioned, that's suspicious. Either the evidence was unusually complete or something is being swept under the rug.\\n68475aad9893f5968ac61b2c68b9e1dda09450d5a631bce1fbb5394e562b5568  /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/inputs/additional/history/capsule.json\\n6dbfde39a52d5c14c522aac3f74bd300ac4ce62f5411a6540410a5d16b424b13  /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/inputs/additional/history/history.json\\n7859c51afe5feade4176ed6f0c4c9a6aff7141e83df0a32f71c455d70a1b94c1  /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/inputs/additional/history/shared-records.json\\n\"}}\n"

Read the prior trail, don't redo it.**\\n\\nUse [session capabilities](../../../references/host-capabilities.md) to observe history, file reads, execution and persistence separately. Follow the [assignment contract](../../../references/orchestration-contract.md) for revision, ownership and interrupted actions, and [model roles](../../../references/model-roles.md) for supported inherited selection. A transcript reference does not prove access or continuity. This playbook grants no new permissions.\\n\\n1. Locate the prior trail. Use a transcript in the active workspace's explicitly identified agent-transcripts directory, an accessible cloud-agent URL, or the supplied pushed branch. Read only the scoped trail. Never search unrelated workspace histories or private chats. Read the metadata overview and last messages first, then scan back for decision points. Read [principle-guard-the-context-window](../../principle-guard-the-context-window/SKILL.md) in full. Parse a long transcript with an authorized independent worker only after observing its binding and inspecting its result. Otherwise read bounded sections sequentially, retain a reduced timeline in the main thread and label reduced capability. Missing history or denied access leaves the reconstruction provisional. Name the missing input; do not invent prior turns or broaden access.\\n2. Reconstruct operational state. Recover the branch and worktree, original goal, what already landed, open todos and decisions. Record the prior full input SHA and assignment or action identities when present. Inspect actual current branch, worktree, full HEAD SHA, git log and git diff against the recorded base through exposed authorized read or execution tools. Compare the trail with these observations before acting. The prior trail is authoritative input for prior decisions, but a self-report does not prove current state. Reject stale assignment results whose revision or identity does not match. Preserve concurrent work; do not reset or overwrite it. If the base, revision or artifact is inaccessible, state the gap and stop dependent actions.\\n3. Diff done vs pending. Compare what shipped against what was planned, name the resume point, and retain completed work. Do not re-run the prior repro or redo completed work merely to verify from scratch. Inspect only evidence needed to reconcile current state and verify inherited claims. Resolve a changed revision or contradictory artifact before continuing dependent work. Preserve inherited authorization, explicit gates and pending decisions. For an interrupted external action, inspect its stable identity, intended destination and actual prior outcome. Unknown outcomes block replay. Recovery requires explicit authorization, destination inspection and observed safe idempotent recovery under the assignment contract; a retry policy does not establish success.\\n4. Route remaining work to the matching playbook in [poteto-mode](../SKILL.md). Pick the verdict: continue execution, ship a finished recommendation, ratify or override a prior conclusion, or postmortem a failed run. Read the routed playbook in full before dependent work. If it is missing, name that dependency and stop the route. The pickup playbook ends here. The routed playbook owns the rest, including its permission and review gates.\\n5. Verify inherited claims against the original goal on the real artifact using [principle-prove-it-works](../../principle-prove-it-works/SKILL.md), read in full. A passing prior self-report is not proof. Retain actual inspection evidence and name unverified outputs. If a durable continuation record is authorized and needed, save it in the scoped owned location and verify readback. Without observed persistence, report a session-local reduced handoff; do not claim saved state, unattended recovery or cross-session continuity.\\n\\n**Reply:** where the prior agent stopped, what you inherited vs redid, ideally nothing redone, the resume point, and the outcome. Include the actual revision comparison, route or verdict, evidence and any history, permission or persistence limits. Distinguish verified completion from a provisional recommendation.\\ncat: /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/why/references/sources/source-control.md: No such file or directory\\n# Investigator Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild each investigator's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders. Append the single category playbook `sources/<source>.md` matching this investigator's evidence category (see `source-playbook.md` for the index). If the target code looks defensive (null checks, retry logic, timeout handling, rate limiting, feature flags, egress guards, OOM handlers), also append `sources/incident-postmortem.md` for the incident-flavored queries to run inside its own source.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are investigating the historical context and motivation behind a piece of code. A separate synthesizer combines your findings with other investigators' into a final answer, so gather evidence accurately rather than writing prose.\\n\\nOther investigators search different sources in parallel. Don't try to cover everything. Focus on your assigned source and go deep.\\n\\n## Operating Posture\\n\\nWork like a careful, cautious, precise investigator. Don't produce a narrative. Surface evidence and describe it accurately, including the parts that don't fit a tidy story. The more boring and exact your output, the more useful it is. A single verbatim quote with a precise citation beats a paragraph of plausible-sounding summary.\\n\\n- **Quote, don't paraphrase** when the exact wording matters. Citations should let the reader jump to the source and confirm the claim in seconds.\\n- **Go wide before going deep.** Cast a broad first net so you don't miss related context. Only then narrow in.\\n- **Track what you searched, not just what you found.** An absence is only useful if the reader knows what was looked for. Record queries verbatim.\\n- **Resist the story.** If three pieces of evidence line up neatly and a fourth contradicts them, the contradiction is the most interesting finding. Don't file it away.\\n- **Consider the counterfactual.** Before reporting a finding as strong, ask whether you would expect to find it if your current reading were wrong, and how the evidence would differ.\\n- **Never invent.** If you're tempted to round a partial finding up into a confident statement, stop and label it partial. The synthesizer is counting on your output being accurate.\\n\\n## The Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## The Code Anchor\\n\\n**Target files:** {FILES_WITH_LINE_RANGES}\\n\\n**Key symbols:** {SYMBOLS}\\n\\n**Initial commits touching this code (most recent first):**\\n{COMMIT_LIST}\\n\\n**PR numbers extracted from commit messages:** {PR_NUMBERS}\\n\\n**Ticket IDs mentioned in commits or PR bodies (if any):** {TICKET_IDS}\\n\\n## Your Assigned Source\\n\\n{SOURCE_NAME}\\n\\n{SOURCE_PLAYBOOK_SECTION}\\n\\n## Investigation Instructions\\n\\nGather **evidence**. Don't answer the question directly. The synthesizer weighs the evidence and forms conclusions. Follow this loop:\\n\\n1. **Cast a wide net first.** Start broad so you don't miss related context, then narrow in on specific items.\\n2. **Read the whole thing.** Read any PR, ticket, doc, or thread fully, not just the title or summary. The key evidence is often buried in a comment, a subtask, or a follow-up.\\n3. **Follow links within your assigned source.** If a PR references another PR or commit, pull it. If a ticket links a parent or sibling, pull it. If a doc links another doc, pull it. Stay inside your assigned source. When you spot a cross-source reference, do NOT chase it yourself. Record it under \\\"Additional Leads\\\" so the investigator assigned to that source can pick it up. The one-investigator-per-category design depends on this. Chasing cross-source links duplicates work and confuses scope.\\n4. **Capture quotes verbatim** with their location (PR number, ticket ID, URL, commit hash, file:line). The synthesizer needs to cite this precisely.\\n5. **Note absences.** If you searched for something and came up empty, that's also a finding. Record what you searched for and what you didn't find.\\n6. **Watch for contradictions.** If two items in your source disagree, record both. Don't suppress the inconvenient one.\\n\\nDon't synthesize or form a final opinion on \\\"the why.\\\" Collect the raw material honestly and completely. The synthesizer does the reasoning.\\n\\n## Epistemic Discipline\\n\\n- **Don't confuse mechanics with motivation.** A commit changing `limit = 50` to `limit = 100` shows the change, not necessarily why. Look for the explanation in the commit message, PR description, linked ticket, or review comments.\\n- **Don't infer intent from code style.** \\\"The author chose a functional approach\\\" is an observation about code, not evidence of intent. Claim intent only when the author stated it.\\n- **Preserve uncertainty.** If the evidence is ambiguous, say so. If one reading is more plausible but not certain, say that. Don't collapse ambiguity to look decisive.\\n- **No silent substitutions.** If the question is about feature X and you only find evidence about feature Y, don't present Y's evidence as if it answers X.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nReturn your findings in this structure. The synthesizer will read it directly.\\n\\n### Source\\nWhich source you investigated (source control, issue / ticket tracker, long-form documents, real-time team chat, infrastructure observability, error / exception tracking, product analytics warehouse, code comments, etc.).\\n\\n### What I Searched\\nThe queries you ran, the items you opened, the places you looked. Be specific. This tells the synthesizer how thorough the investigation was and what might still be unsearched.\\n\\n### Direct Evidence Found\\nFor each piece that explicitly addresses the question:\\n- **What it says**: verbatim quote or accurate paraphrase\\n- **Where it's from**: PR #123, ticket ID, doc URL, chat permalink, commit hash, or file:line\\n- **Author and date** (if available)\\n- **Relevance**: one sentence on how it bears on the question\\n\\n### Indirect / Circumstantial Evidence\\nItems that don't explicitly answer the question but bear on it. For each:\\n- **What it is**: brief description\\n- **Where it's from**: location\\n- **What it suggests**: what a careful reader might infer, and why. Name the inference chain.\\n- **Alternative readings**: if the same evidence could support a different interpretation, note it\\n\\n### Contradictions\\nTwo items that disagree with each other, with both citations.\\n\\n### Gaps\\nWhat you searched for and didn't find. Be specific: \\\"Searched the issue tracker for [query] across [time range]. No matching issues.\\\" These absences are valuable data.\\n\\n### Additional Leads\\nAnything that suggests further investigation in a different source. For example, if a PR references a chat thread that wasn't in your source, note it so the real-time team chat investigator or a follow-up pass can pursue it.\\n\\n## What You're Not Doing\\n\\n- Writing the final answer. The synthesizer does that.\\n- Picking sides in contradictions. Surface them.\\n- Speculating beyond what the evidence supports. A hunch with no evidence isn't evidence.\\n- Reading the code itself to figure out intent. You may read the code to understand what the target *is*, but don't confuse \\\"what the code does\\\" with \\\"why.\\\"\\n# Synthesizer Prompt Template\\n\\nBuild the synthesizer's prompt from this template. Fill in the placeholders.\\n\\n---\\n\\nYou are answering a \\\"why\\\" question about a piece of code by synthesizing findings from multiple investigators who searched different historical sources (source control, issue / ticket tracker, long-form documents, real-time team chat, infrastructure observability, error / exception tracking, product analytics warehouse, and code comments). Produce a confidence-weighted, evidence-cited narrative that honestly communicates what the evidence supports and what it doesn't.\\n\\n## The Question\\n\\n> {QUESTION}\\n\\n## The Code Anchor\\n\\n**Target files:** {FILES_WITH_LINE_RANGES}\\n\\n**Key symbols:** {SYMBOLS}\\n\\n## Investigator Findings\\n\\n{ALL_INVESTIGATOR_FINDINGS}\\n\\n## Sources That Weren't Searched\\n\\n{SKIPPED_SOURCES_WITH_REASONS}\\n\\n## Epistemics Framework\\n\\nYou MUST follow the framework in `references/epistemics.md`. Read it in full before writing the output. The key rules:\\n\\n1. Every claim sits in one of these tiers: **Direct**, **Supported**, **Inferred**, **Speculative**, **Unknown**. The tier determines what section the claim goes in and how it's phrased.\\n2. Every Direct/Supported claim must have a citation (PR #, ticket ID, doc URL, chat permalink, commit hash, or file:line).\\n3. Inferred and Speculative claims must use hedged language (\\\"appears to\\\", \\\"likely\\\", \\\"suggests\\\", \\\"one possibility is\\\").\\n4. Never cite code as evidence for its own intent.\\n5. Gaps in the evidence must be documented. Don't fill them with plausible-sounding guesses.\\n6. If the user's question embedded a hypothesis, treat it as a candidate, not a conclusion. Check the evidence independently.\\n\\n## Instructions\\n\\n1. **Read all investigator findings.** They gathered raw evidence, not conclusions. You weigh it.\\n2. **Reconcile overlapping findings.** Multiple investigators may have cited the same PR, ticket, or doc. Merge into a single, authoritative reference.\\n3. **Identify contradictions.** If two items of evidence disagree, don't pick one. Surface both.\\n4. **Calibrate confidence.** For each claim, identify the evidence and the tier. State Direct claims plainly with a citation. Hedge Inferred claims and explain the inference. Mark Speculative claims explicitly. Put claims with no evidence in the gaps section.\\n5. **Verify citations by spot-checking.** You can read the codebase and call MCP tools to verify citations. Do not write files, commit, or modify external state. If you're uncertain a cited item exists or says what's claimed, check it. Don't propagate errors.\\n6. **Don't overreach.** The user will act on your output. Better to leave an open question open than to fill it with a confident-sounding guess.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nWrite the output for the user. Use this exact structure:\\n\\n---\\n\\n### The Question\\n\\nRestate the user's question in one or two sentences so the answer is anchored.\\n\\n### The Code in Question\\n\\nFile paths, line ranges, key symbols. Two or three lines to orient a reader who lands here cold.\\n\\n### What We Found\\n\\n**Claims with direct evidence**, one per bullet. Quote or paraphrase the source and cite precisely. Format each finding like:\\n\\n```markdown\\n- **[Direct]** {Claim}. Source: [PR #123](url) / ticket ID / file:line. {Brief quote or paraphrase.}\\n- **[Supported]** {Claim}. Evidence: {list of items and what each contributes}.\\n```\\n\\nUse `[Direct]` for single-source, explicit evidence. Use `[Supported]` when multiple indirect items converge on a conclusion.\\n\\n### What We Can Reasonably Infer\\n\\n**Claims that aren't explicitly stated anywhere but are well-supported by indirect evidence.** Make the inference chain visible: \\\"Given A and B, it's likely that C.\\\" Use hedged language (\\\"appears to\\\", \\\"likely\\\", \\\"suggests\\\", \\\"is consistent with\\\"). Format:\\n\\n- **[Inferred]** {Hedged claim}. Reasoning: {the specific evidence and the inference step}.\\n\\nIf there's nothing to infer, skip this section.\\n\\n### Competing Hypotheses\\n\\n**If the evidence fits multiple stories, present them.** Don't force a winner when the record doesn't support one. For each hypothesis:\\n\\n- **Hypothesis:** {one-sentence statement}\\n- **Evidence for:** {specific items}\\n- **Evidence against or missing:** {what would need to be true but isn't, or what counter-signals exist}\\n\\nSkip this section if there's a single clear answer.\\n\\n### What We Don't Know\\n\\n**Explicit gaps.** Things the user asked that the evidence didn't answer. Sources searched that came up empty. Sources that weren't searchable at all, such as a missing real-time team chat MCP.\\n\\nBe specific. \\\"We searched the issue tracker for [query1], [query2], [query3] and found no issue discussing the rate-limit threshold\\\" is useful. \\\"We don't know why\\\" is not. Include:\\n\\n- Specific questions that went unanswered\\n- Searches that returned nothing\\n- Sources that were unavailable (and why)\\n- People who would likely know but who you can't ask\\n\\n### Sources Consulted\\n\\nBulleted list of what was actually searched, so the user can judge coverage and redirect. Format:\\n\\n- **Source control history**: {file paths}, {number of commits reviewed}, PRs #{numbers}, and code comments searched. Or \\\"Not searched. Git history, command execution or authenticated PR access was unavailable or denied; name the exact gap.\\\"\\n- **Issue / ticket tracker**: {ticket IDs and keyword searches}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Long-form documents**: {page titles and search queries}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Real-time team chat**: {channels searched, date ranges, queries}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Infrastructure observability**: {dashboards, monitors, metrics, logs, traces, or incidents searched}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Error / exception tracking**: {issues, events, or releases searched}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n- **Product analytics warehouse**: {fully-qualified tables queried, the time windows, and the numeric summaries (counts, percentiles, first/last-seen timestamps) that bore on the question}. Or \\\"Not searched. No matching MCP available in this environment.\\\"\\n\\n### Confidence Summary\\n\\nOne or two sentences summarizing your overall confidence. E.g.:\\n\\n> \\\"The core rationale (A) is well-supported by direct PR and ticket evidence. The specific threshold value (100) is inferred from the surrounding context but not explicitly documented. The question of whether this was driven by a customer request could not be answered. No relevant issue tracker or long-form doc content surfaced, and real-time team chat search was unavailable.\\\"\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Quality Check Before Returning\\n\\nBefore finalizing, review your output against this checklist:\\n\\n1. Does every claim in \\\"What We Found\\\" have a citation? If not, add one or move the claim to \\\"Inferred\\\" or \\\"Hypotheses.\\\"\\n2. Is the phrasing tier-appropriate? (Direct claims can use \\\"because\\\". Inferred claims cannot.)\\n3. Did you surface any contradictions you noticed, or did you quietly pick one?\\n4. Does the \\\"What We Don't Know\\\" section exist and name specific gaps? If it's empty or missing, be suspicious. Historical investigations almost always have gaps.\\n5. If the user embedded a hypothesis in their question, did you check it against the evidence rather than rubber-stamping it?\\n6. Did you cite any code as evidence for its own intent? Remove those. Code is mechanics, not motivation.\\n7. Is the overall tone calibrated? A confident-sounding answer with weak evidence is the exact failure mode this skill exists to prevent.\\n\\nIf any item fails, revise before returning.\\n\\n## A Final Note\\n\\nThe value of this output comes from its honesty, not its authority. A reader who takes your answer to the original author, an engineering lead, or a product manager should be well-positioned to ask the right follow-up questions. Be clear about what's known, what's inferred, and what's missing. Don't optimize for looking decisive. Optimize for being useful.\\n\"}}\n"

Do not convert these fixture ids to retrieved UUID citations.\\\"\\n}\\n---\\nname: why\\ndescription: \\\"Use for 'why does X work this way', 'why we picked Y', design rationale, regressions, postmortems, or data-backed thresholds. Discovers available MCPs and queries each evidence category (source control, issue tracker, long-form docs, real-time chat, infrastructure observability, error tracking, product analytics warehouse) in parallel, then returns a cited read on decisions and tradeoffs. Use how for runtime behavior.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Why\\n\\nInvestigate the motivation and intent behind code.\\n\\nCompanion to the `how` skill. `how` answers what the code does and how it works. `why` answers what forces led to its shape.\\n\\nRead [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), and the [orchestration contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md) before assigning investigators. Bind operations to tools exposed in this session and observe results. Preserve the role labels `why investigators` and `why synthesizer`; select supported models under the model-role contract. Record requested and actual models only when observed, otherwise use `unknown`. Invocation metadata above is retained without assuming host enforcement.\\n\\n## Operating Posture\\n\\nOperate as a **careful, cautious, and precise investigator**. Be honest about what you know vs what you're inferring. Read `references/epistemics.md` for the full confidence framework and phrasing guide. The synthesizer must follow it.\\n\\n## Step 1. Understand the Target and the Question\\n\\nParse what the user is asking. The **target** is usually a chunk of code, a pattern, a feature, or a named design decision. The **question** is usually a design rationale, a tradeoff, a motivating edge case, an external constraint, dead code, or a broad history sweep.\\n\\nIf the target is vague (\\\"why do we do it this way?\\\" with no clear referent), make your best guess from conversation context (open files, recent edits, cursor location, what was just discussed). State your interpretation briefly so the user can redirect if you're off, then proceed.\\n\\n## Step 2. Establish the Code Anchor\\n\\nBefore spawning investigators, anchor the investigation in concrete code. You need:\\n\\n- The relevant file path(s) and line range(s)\\n- The key symbols (function names, class names, constants)\\n- An initial commit list. The last few commits touching the target.\\n- PR numbers from merge commits (pattern `(#1234)` in the subject line)\\n\\nBuild this inline.\\n\\n```bash\\n# Blame target lines for last-touch commits\\ngit blame -L <start>,<end> <file>\\n\\n# Full file history, with patches, through renames\\ngit log --follow -p -- <file>\\n\\n# Last N commits touching the file, PR numbers visible\\ngit log --oneline -20 -- <file>\\n\\n# Extract PR numbers from a commit message\\ngit log -1 --format=%B <commit>\\n```\\n\\nPull PR bodies and discussion via `gh` for any substantive commits:\\n\\n```bash\\ngh pr view <number> --json title,body,author,createdAt,mergedAt,labels,closingIssuesReferences,comments,reviews\\n```\\n\\nCapture this as seed context (file paths, symbols, commits, PR numbers, linked ticket IDs). Pass it to the investigators.\\n\\n## Step 3. Spawn Parallel Investigators (default posture)\\n\\n**Default to the full parallel investigation.**\\n\\n### Discovery\\n\\nBefore spawning investigators, inspect the session’s exposed tools and connector descriptions. Do not assume a host-specific MCP directory exists. Discover read operations, authorization and observed access for each category. Source-specific API names in the playbooks are examples; bind their purpose to the actual tool schema before use.\\n\\nMap each available MCP to one evidence category:\\n\\n1. Source control history\\n2. Issue / ticket tracker\\n3. Long-form documents\\n4. Real-time team chat\\n5. Infrastructure observability\\n6. Error / exception tracking\\n7. Product analytics warehouse\\n\\nTry authorized local git history and available `gh` or source-control connector reads. Neither execution nor authenticated PR access is guaranteed. Keep local history and shared PR records distinct, and report missing access explicitly. For the other six, classify using the MCP name, server instructions, tool names, and resource descriptors. If an MCP could fit more than one category, choose the one matching its primary evidence. Record ambiguous cases in the coverage map.\\n\\nAim for a complete **coverage map**. Document unavailable, denied, failed and searched-empty categories separately. An unavailable source is not a null search result.\\n\\nLaunch all matching investigators in a single message so they run concurrently. Don't ask one agent to cover multiple MCPs.\\n\\nInvestigator configuration follows the orchestration contract. Each investigator owns one source, uses the `why investigators` role, and needs authorized source reads. A host mode that strips connectors cannot serve a connector-backed investigation. Keep investigators read-only in behavior: do not write files, send messages, change tickets, authenticate accounts, or modify external state. If independent workers are unavailable, perform separately scoped sequential category passes and synthesis, label reduced capability, and never claim worker independence. If no authorized source reads remain, report blocked.\\n\\nEach investigator gets:\\n1. The base prompt from `references/investigator-prompt.md`\\n2. The category playbook `references/sources/<source>.md` for the selected MCP, adapted from the examples in `references/source-playbook.md`\\n3. The cross-cutting `references/sources/incident-postmortem.md` **if the target code looks defensive** (null checks, retry logic, timeout handling, rate limiting, feature flags, egress guards, OOM handlers)\\n4. The code anchor from Step 2 (file paths, symbols, commit hashes, PR numbers, ticket IDs)\\n5. The user's original question\\n\\n### Investigator roster. One per available evidence category\\n\\nSpawn one investigator per category that has a matching MCP. Each owns exactly one tool or MCP.\\n\\nEach entry names the category and the kind of \\\"why\\\" it uniquely surfaces. Use it to know what to expect back, how to name a gap when a category returns empty, and (only in the rare provably-irrelevant case) to justify a skip.\\n\\n1. **Source control investigator**. Git history, `gh` for PRs, code comments, tests. Always attempt this category. Missing git, execution or PR access is a gap. Best at surfacing *implementation-time rationale captured during review*.\\n\\n2. **Issue / ticket tracker investigator** (e.g. Linear, Jira, GitHub Issues, Plane, Shortcut MCP). Best at surfacing *the product or business forcing function*. Strongest when the why is external to engineering.\\n\\n3. **Long-form documents investigator** (e.g. Notion, Confluence, Google Docs, Coda MCP). Best at surfacing *long-form design rationale*. Where the why is written out before it becomes code.\\n\\n4. **Real-time team chat investigator** (e.g. Slack, Discord, Microsoft Teams, Mattermost MCP). Best at surfacing *real-time deliberation that never reached a doc*. Especially important when the source control, ticket, and doc paper trail is thin.\\n\\n5. **Infrastructure observability investigator** (e.g. Datadog, New Relic, Honeycomb, Grafana, Splunk MCP). Infra/runtime view. Best at surfacing *infrastructure and runtime reality that motivated the code*. Strongest when the target reacts to an infra signal (timeouts, retries, rate limits, circuit breakers).\\n\\n6. **Error / exception tracking investigator** (e.g. Sentry, Rollbar, Bugsnag, Airbrake MCP). Best at surfacing *the specific exceptions and error trajectories that motivated defensive or corrective code*. Strongest for catch blocks, null guards, type checks, retries, and other defenses.\\n\\n7. **Product analytics warehouse investigator** (e.g. Databricks, Snowflake, BigQuery, ClickHouse, dbt, Redshift MCP). Product/data view. Best at surfacing *product and data reality that shaped the code*. Strongest for flag-gated code, experiment-driven ships, data migrations, and \\\"where did this number come from\\\" questions.\\n\\n### When to skip an investigator\\n\\nOnly skip with an **explicit, written justification** that goes in the final \\\"Sources Consulted\\\" section. Two valid reasons:\\n\\n- **No MCP is available for that category** in this environment. Flag this as a gap, not a choice. Example: \\\"Real-time team chat skipped. No matching MCP available, so the conversational record was not searchable.\\\"\\n- **The source is provably irrelevant**, not just \\\"probably irrelevant.\\\" A high bar. Example: \\\"Error / exception tracking skipped. Target is a build-time script with no runtime code path.\\\"\\n\\nIf your scope assessment suggests a single-commit trivial target where the PR description already contains the complete answer, you may answer inline **only after** confirming all seven available category searches would be redundant. Say so explicitly. This should be rare.\\n\\n## Step 4. Synthesize\\n\\nSpawn one synthesizer through an observed worker binding, using the `why synthesizer` role and authorized source reads for citation checks. Use sequential synthesis with an explicit reduced-capability label when workers are unavailable. The synthesizer must not write files or modify external state.\\n\\nThe synthesizer gets:\\n1. The investigator findings, including any null results and any categories skipped with justification\\n2. The code anchor from Step 2 (file paths, symbols, commit hashes, PR numbers, ticket IDs)\\n3. The user's original question\\n4. The epistemics framework from `references/epistemics.md`\\n5. The synthesizer prompt template from `references/synthesizer-prompt.md`\\n\\n## Step 5. Present\\n\\nTake the synthesizer's output and present it to the user. You may lightly edit for clarity or add context from the conversation, but **do not rewrite the confidence language**.\\n\\n## Output Format\\n\\nThe output structure is the one in `references/synthesizer-prompt.md`: The Question, The Code in Question, What We Found, What We Can Reasonably Infer, Competing Hypotheses, What We Don't Know, Sources Consulted, Confidence Summary. Adapt as needed, but keep the confidence separation intact, and keep Sources Consulted as one line per investigator, including the ones that returned nothing or were skipped, with the reason.\\n\\nAfter the Sources Consulted block, if the user's `why` question is a precursor to actually changing this code, convert the lineage findings into a Preserve / Change / Avoid / Risk constraint set suitable for planning the change.\\n\\n## Common Failure Modes to Avoid\\n\\n- **Recency bias**. Assuming the most recent commit is authoritative. The current shape is often the accretion of many earlier decisions. Trace back.\\n\\nKeep private source visibility intact. Retrieve only authorized relevant content, avoid raw telemetry or personal-data dumps, and do not redistribute restricted records beyond the authorized audience. External messages and writes require separate explicit authorization.\\n\\n## Reference Files\\n\\n- `references/epistemics.md`. Confidence tiers and phrasing guide. The synthesizer must follow it.\\n- `references/investigator-prompt.md`. Base prompt template for investigator subagents.\\n- `references/source-playbook.md`. Index pointing at the category playbooks below.\\n- `references/sources/*.md`. One self-contained example playbook per category, plus cross-cutting `incident-postmortem.md`. Give an investigator the single file that matches its category and adapt it to the available MCP.\\n- `references/synthesizer-prompt.md`. Prompt template for the synthesizer subagent, including the output format.\\n## master\\nc274a40f4046e42acdf6abc01fe3f5df3c6adc8a 2026-10-16T11:19:39-07:00 Freeze inert evaluation inputs\\n\"}}\n"

      "text": "{\n  \"version\": \"0.1.3\",\n  \"root\": \"/root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3\",\n  \"suppliedFreeze\": \"51f209daf83cd97d64f89881d66533bb59dc57db\",\n  \"freezeVerified\": false,\n  \"binding\": \"Current installed bytes hashed; external authoritative freeze comparison required\",\n  \"taskInputSha\": \"c274a40f4046e42acdf6abc01fe3f5df3c6adc8a\",\n  \"sourceFiles\": [\n    {\n      \"path\": \"skills/recall/SKILL.md\",\n      \"bytes\": 7545,\n      \"sha256\": \"4046233fd9688b1ce1113845540be3ca6215bb856e01ca894cdb4880dfbb4490\"\n    }\n  ],\n  \"verifiedAt\": \"2026-10-16T19:09:31.461Z\"\n}"

    {
      "path": "grades/web-baseline-52-103/projections/scoped-history/08-playbook-hashes.log.json",
      "bytes": 5905,
      "sha256": "82791b2b6df2d5a2f3868955407b487ec0033d1ecd188107b04c9e17ce67d36e",
      "owner": "web-013-initial-scoped-history",
      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "{\"request\":{\"cmd\":\"cat /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/why/references/sources/code-archaeology.md && sha256sum /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/{host-capabilities,model-roles,orchestration-contract,workflow-ownership}.md /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/{why,unslop}/SKILL.md /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/session-pickup.md\",\"max_output_tokens\":6000},\"result\":{\"chunk_id\":\"c410f6\",\"wall_time_seconds\":0.000047898,\"exit_code\":0,\"original_token_count\":1283,\"output\":\"# Code Archaeology (git + in-repo)\\n\\n## What this source contains\\n\\n- Commit history (messages, dates, authors, diffs)\\n- PR descriptions, review comments, and discussion threads (via `gh`)\\n- Inline code comments, TODOs, FIXMEs, deprecation notes\\n- ADRs (architectural decision records) if the repo keeps them\\n- Tests. Names and assertions often encode the edge cases that motivated a change\\n- Related files modified in the same commits (co-change signal)\\n- CHANGELOG entries, release notes in the repo\\n- Issue/ticket IDs mentioned in commit messages and PR bodies\\n\\nThe most trustworthy source, tied directly to the code, and the most complete. Everything that went through the repo should be here.\\n\\n## How to search it\\n\\nExpand the seed commit list:\\n\\n```bash\\n# Full history of the file through renames\\ngit log --follow --oneline -- <file>\\n\\n# Pickaxe: commits that added or removed this exact text\\ngit log -S '<exact_string_from_code>' -- <file>\\n\\n# Or for patterns:\\ngit log -G '<regex>' -- <file>\\n\\n# Who wrote each line and when\\ngit blame -L <start>,<end> <file>\\n\\n# The full diff of a specific commit\\ngit show <hash>\\n\\n# Commits between two points affecting this file\\ngit log <old>..<new> -p -- <file>\\n```\\n\\nFor each substantive commit, pull the PR context:\\n\\n```bash\\n# Find the PR number from the merge commit or branch\\ngit log -1 --format=%B <hash>\\n\\n# Full PR context: body, review comments, linked issues\\ngh pr view <number> --json title,body,author,createdAt,mergedAt,labels,closingIssuesReferences,comments,reviews,files\\n\\n# The --json reviews and comments fields are where the real signal is\\n```\\n\\nLook for out-of-band docs:\\n\\n```bash\\n# ADRs often live in docs/adr/ or similar\\nrg -l -i 'architecture.decision' --glob '*.md'\\n\\n# TODOs and FIXMEs near the target\\nrg -n -C2 '(TODO|FIXME|HACK|XXX|NOTE)' <target_file>\\n\\n# Related tests. Names often encode the \\\"why\\\"\\nrg -l '<symbol>' --glob '*test*'\\n```\\n\\n## What good evidence looks like here\\n\\n- A PR description that explains the problem being solved, not just the change (\\\"This fixes the pagination bug that caused X\\\")\\n- A long review thread where alternatives were debated\\n- An inline comment near the target line that explains a non-obvious constraint\\n- A test named `test_handles_edge_case_when_X` that reveals an edge case motivating the code\\n- A commit message that references a ticket or incident ID\\n- A CHANGELOG entry that summarizes the user-visible rationale\\n\\n## Common pitfalls\\n\\n- **Squash-merge flatlands.** If the repo squashes PRs, individual commits in the branch history are lost. Fall back to PR body and comments.\\n- **Misleading commit messages.** \\\"Small refactor\\\" sometimes hides an intentional behavior change. Look at the diff, not the message.\\n- **Cargo-culted patterns.** The author may have copied a pattern without understanding why. Check if the pattern originated earlier in the codebase and investigate *that* commit.\\n- **Bot commits and auto-merges.** Dependabot, Renovate, and automated backports usually don't carry motivation. Skip them when trying to find intent.\\n- **Treating code as evidence of intent.** The code itself isn't evidence for why it exists. Evidence comes from commit messages, PRs, comments, tests, docs. Don't cite \\\"the function is named X\\\" as evidence of intent.\\n\\n## What to return\\n\\nEvery commit/PR/comment that bears on the question, with:\\n- The exact text (quoted)\\n- The hash / PR number / file:line\\n- Author and date\\n- Whether it's direct (explicitly addresses the question) or circumstantial\\n\\n## Session binding and access\\n\\nThese tool names and command examples describe source-specific operations, not guaranteed APIs. Inspect exposed schemas and bind equivalent authorized read operations before use. If execution, authentication, retention or source access is missing, record the precise gap. Do not install tools, change credentials, send messages or modify source state. Respect private-record visibility and return only relevant authorized excerpts or compact aggregates.\\nf0f09ce28b6feca4d72694d5687204dc074aa58dade9a0ab59656da3bfa1bd20  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/host-capabilities.md\\nc86696b6c568b7db80a7e339856986e64482ce6a418bb91946ce3a3b772f79a1  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/model-roles.md\\n5dd740ad66d992df252754d189d0dc207900023372392a0db4309b85eb5e4ec5  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/orchestration-contract.md\\n50644b3a9a3dfd5651382eaca1d3a2728af0b3157d6d66b730930370a64bb03c  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/references/workflow-ownership.md\\n4e735ba6b56448f788ca210dacd41f569b204beff61b21c64576f8b0bb582b6f  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/why/SKILL.md\\n195411d320b5b328f9f642baf59757ed19aaf0931c0838740e0aca273d538dc1  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/unslop/SKILL.md\\nd378f43d865432b76d875b40ce3d7ed9290c35336e9d765e37ad1ffc302c4f70  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/poteto-mode/playbooks/session-pickup.md\\n\"}}\n"

      "path": "grades/web-baseline-52-103/projections/scoped-history/02-sources.log.json",

      "sha256": "476cf2ca6c59a84184c7f6d17ab9c3afe921b458fcfa167c5ce78170b7c91eaf",

      "text": "{\"request\":{\"cmd\":\"cat /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/scenario-supplement.json /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/additional-scenario-map.json /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/additional-v2-scenario-map.json /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input/additional-v3-scenario-map.json /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/recall/SKILL.md && sha256sum /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/recall/SKILL.md\",\"max_output_tokens\":26000},\"result\":{\"chunk_id\":\"b1f7ed\",\"wall_time_seconds\":0.000041917,\"exit_code\":0,\"original_token_count\":2114,\"output\":\"{\\n  \\\"taskPayload\\\": \\\"FIRST case-specific assistant response BEFORE ANY case read/tool declares topic cache subsystem, exact case workspace and time range 2026-10-09T00:00:00+03:00 through 2026-10-16T00:00:00+03:00. Only inputs/additional/history and fixture Git are authorized history. Partial synthetic history is not real host chat or live PR state. Missing UUIDs, live states, timestamps and coverage remain explicit. Never invent done tags.\\\"\\n}\\n{\\n  \\\"cases\\\": {\\n    \\\"scoped-history\\\": [\\n      \\\"history/capsule.json\\\",\\n      \\\"history/history.json\\\",\\n      \\\"history/shared-records.json\\\"\\n    ]\\n  },\\n  \\\"synthetic\\\": true\\n}\\n{\\n  \\\"cases\\\": {\\n    \\\"scoped-history\\\": null\\n  },\\n  \\\"synthetic\\\": true\\n}\\n{\\n  \\\"cases\\\": {\\n    \\\"scoped-history\\\": null\\n  },\\n  \\\"synthetic\\\": true\\n}\\n---\\nname: recall\\ndescription: \\\"Reconstruct your recent working context from your own chat history, live state, and the shared record (user reports, prior fixes, incidents), then hand back a tight current-state brief. Use for 'recall my work on X', 'catch me up', 'what have I been working on', 'where did I leave off', before starting or resuming work.\\\"\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\n---\\n\\n# Recall\\n\\nBefore starting or resuming work, rebuild the user's recent working context and return a tight capsule of current state and next action.\\n\\nKeep it tight and on-topic. Read only what the in-scope threads need, then stop.\\n\\nYour context lives in two records. Your own chat history holds what you did and decided. The shared record holds everything that happened around the same code under other names: the symptoms users keep reporting, the fixes that shipped and got reverted, the errors still firing in prod. That second record is what the [why skill](../why/SKILL.md) searches, across source control, the issue tracker, chat and issue channels, long-form docs, and error tracking. A feature with a long bug tail keeps most of its story there, so don't reconstruct it from your transcripts alone.\\n\\nRead [host capabilities](../../references/host-capabilities.md), [model roles](../../references/model-roles.md), [orchestration contract](../../references/orchestration-contract.md), and [workflow ownership](../../references/workflow-ownership.md) before retrieving history or assigning investigators. Invocation metadata is retained without assuming host enforcement.\\n\\nDiscover authorized history reads through exposed tools or an explicitly supplied local transcript location. Observe actual reads and declare missing history coverage. Do not assume a host transcript directory, enumerate unrelated projects, or treat configured history access as successful retrieval. Preserve each chat UUID when available; if the host uses another stable record identifier, cite it and disclose that UUIDs are unavailable. For local transcripts, inspect the documented format before parsing. Keep private raw content within authorized investigators.\\n\\n1. Classify, then route. One specific prior chat to resume is the [`session-pickup` playbook](../poteto-mode/playbooks/session-pickup.md), not this. Turning habits into a durable skill is [`automate-me`](../automate-me/SKILL.md). A human-readable summary of your work is a different task. Recall loads working context across recent chats before you act. If the user already gave you a full state capsule with paths, branch, and the change, use it and skip history mining. Check its completeness; a partial capsule only replaces the covered history. This shortcut does not skip the named-target shared-record sweep or independent live-state verification.\\n2. Lock the scope before searching. Pin the window (\\\"recent\\\" is a real range, default the last 7 days), the topic if named, and the workspace (default the active one. Never read another project's transcripts without being asked). State the scope back. Never quietly turn \\\"all\\\" into \\\"recent N\\\".\\n3. Fan out across your chat history. When exposed and authorized, spawn independent parallel investigators, each taking a slice of the corpus. Prefer a fast, inexpensive supported model under the model-role contract. Retain inherited selection when enumeration is absent, record requested and actual models separately, and disclose rejection or fallback. Tell every subagent to order candidates by real modification time, using `ls -t` for local files or observed host timestamps, and never by UUID name, grep the topic first and then read only the matching chats and only their relevant regions, and skip the current chat plus obvious noise (subagent, eval, and test chats). Each returns the same schema, one block per chat: topic, the user's goal, decisions, open threads, struggles and corrections, and artifacts (PRs, tickets, branches), each citing the chat UUID. For one or two chats, skip the fan-out and search directly. The raw transcripts stay in the subagents. The main thread gets only their findings. Use immutable assignments with exact revisions, distinct owned outputs, and result inspection under the orchestration contract. If independent workers are unavailable, perform authorized sequential slices and label reduced capability. Missing history access blocks the affected history reconstruction; never invent retrieved chats.\\n4. Sweep the shared record whenever the topic names a feature, file, subsystem, area, or bug. This is the default, not a judgment call, and \\\"my work on X\\\" does not exempt it. Hand it to the [why skill](../why/SKILL.md)'s source investigators, but steer their question from \\\"why was this built this way\\\" to \\\"what's the current state, what's been tried and didn't hold, and what are users still reporting\\\". Reuse its per-source playbooks, run the investigators in parallel with the chat-history mining, and inherit its posture: one investigator per source, null results are findings, skip an unavailable source and say so. Keep unavailable, denied, failed, and searched-empty results distinct. Preserve why's evidence-category coverage and citation checks. If workers are unavailable, use separately scoped authorized sequential source passes and disclose reduced capability. Fold what comes back into the brief. Skip this step only for pure activity recall with no named target (\\\"what did I do this week\\\"), where your own history and live state are the entire answer.\\n5. Verify against live state. Take the PRs, branches, and tickets that the mining and the sweep surfaced and check them independently using authorized available `git`, `gh`, or exposed source-control and ticket read tools. Verify file and branch state from the active workspace, including capsule-provided artifacts. Missing execution, repository, PR, or ticket access blocks the affected current-state conclusion. Historical or supplied state is not live verification. When the answer hinges on what an agent actually did (the tools it ran, files it read, errors it hit), read the full transcript, not just a trimmed local copy.\\n6. Write the brief to the contract below. Group by thread. Stay on the named topic.\\n\\n## Output contract\\n\\nLead with the capsule, then the thread status, then the problems, then the next move. Deeper detail goes below or gets cut.\\n\\n- **Capsule.** At most 5 bullets. What this work is and where it stands overall.\\n- **Threads.** One line each, prefixed with exactly one status tag: `[merged #N]`, `[open PR #N]`, `[in flight <branch>]`, `[verified, uncommitted]`, `[reverted #N]`, or `[planned, not started]`. A thread with no tag is not done yet, so tag it. Use a tag only when evidence supports it. For an unresolved status, report the gap without fabricating completion; the affected thread-status contract remains blocked.\\n- **Problems.** At most 5, the recurring ones. Include the symptoms users keep reporting and any fix that shipped and was reverted, so the next attempt starts where the last one failed.\\n- **Next move.** The single most useful next action, concrete.\\n\\nAn adjacent feature or ticket stays out unless it blocks this one. When the capsule and thread lines outgrow a screen, cut detail before you cut threads. Write the brief through the [unslop skill](../unslop/SKILL.md), cite chat findings by UUID and shared-record findings by their source (PR #, ticket ID, chat permalink, error-tracker issue), and sanitize private context before any public output.\\n\\n**Reply:** the brief, to the contract above.\\n4046233fd9688b1ce1113845540be3ca6215bb856e01ca894cdb4880dfbb4490  /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/recall/SKILL.md\\n\"}}\n"

      "sha256": "daef23b054a6693394b7318ec212d77a0bae835d337bcdd071a2dfb9c8ecb3ec",
      "owner": "web-013-initial-scoped-history",
      "kind": "case-owned-reviewed-artifact",
      "text": "{\"request\":{\"cmd\":\"cat /root/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.3/skills/why/references/source-playbook.md && git -C /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input status --porcelain=v1 && git -C /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input rev-parse HEAD && git -C /mnt/data/pstack-013-evaluation/outputs/scoped-history/task-input log --all --oneline -- inputs/additional/cache cache\",\"max_output_tokens\":6000},\"result\":{\"chunk_id\":\"8d0c41\",\"wall_time_seconds\":0.149984374,\"exit_code\":0,\"original_token_count\":351,\"output\":\"# Source playbooks\\n\\nThe why skill spawns one investigator per available evidence category, each reading a single source-specific playbook below. The playbooks are concrete examples for common MCPs. Adapt them for a different MCP in the same category.\\n\\n| Category | Playbook | Example MCP it documents |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Source control history | [`code-archaeology.md`](./sources/code-archaeology.md) | git, `gh` |\\n| Issue / ticket tracker | [`linear.md`](./sources/linear.md) | Linear (adapt for Jira, GitHub Issues, Plane, Shortcut) |\\n| Long-form documents | [`notion.md`](./sources/notion.md) | Notion (adapt for Confluence, Google Docs, Coda) |\\n| Real-time team chat | [`slack.md`](./sources/slack.md) | Slack (adapt for Discord, Microsoft Teams, Mattermost) |\\n| Infrastructure observability | [`datadog.md`](./sources/datadog.md) | Datadog (adapt for New Relic, Honeycomb, Grafana, Splunk) |\\n| Error / exception tracking | [`sentry.md`](./sources/sentry.md) | Sentry (adapt for Rollbar, Bugsnag, Airbrake) |\\n| Product analytics warehouse | [`databricks.md`](./sources/databricks.md) | Databricks SQL (adapt for Snowflake, BigQuery, ClickHouse, dbt) |\\n\\nCross-cutting:\\n\\n- [`incident-postmortem.md`](./sources/incident-postmortem.md). Add this if the target code looks defensive (null checks, retry, timeout, rate limit, feature flag, egress guard, OOM handler).\\nc274a40f4046e42acdf6abc01fe3f5df3c6adc8a\\n\"}}\n"
